Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because the clinic doctor kind of got on me about my eating. She made it sound like it was a bigger deal than I think it is, but she was pretty insistent that I come talk to someone.

I don’t know if I’d call it a “strong fear” exactly, because that sounds so dramatic, but… yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I eat something that feels really heavy — pizza, or one of those big sandwiches from a deli, something like that — I get this really panicky feeling afterward.

It’s not even just about how I look, though that’s part of it, I guess. It’s more like I start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m just… bigger, heavier, slower? Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.

So… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.

I don’t really think of it as, like, “significant,” but my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to. I’m always pulling them up when I’m on a ladder or bending down, which is annoying more than anything.\n\nAnd people at job sites have said stuff — like, “You’re getting too thin,” or they’ll make comments when I’m carrying supplies, like I look smaller or whatever. I don’t know. I notice it, but I don’t see it the same way they do. To me it feels more like I’ve just leaned out because I’m on my feet all day, going up and down ladders, moving equipment, working long hours. So, yeah, maybe lower than before, but I don’t really know if I’d say “much lower than normal.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if it seems like you're already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a “strong fear” exactly, because that sounds so dramatic, but… yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I eat something that feels really heavy — pizza, or one of those big sandwiches from a deli, something like that — I get this really panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s not even just about how I look, though that’s part of it, I guess. It’s more like I start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m just… bigger, heavier, slower? Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.\n\nSo… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever do anything after eating — like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives — to try to control your weight or keep from gaining weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / painter_or_paperhanger / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.

I mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.

I don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.

Like, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.

And I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.

I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.

But I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.

I don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.

I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.\n\nLike, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.

But… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.

I just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. But I can understand that maybe other people would say I put too much importance on it. I don’t know if I fully agree with that, but I can see why you’re asking.

It doesn’t feel that unusual to me, but I guess that’s what she was concerned about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you consider your weight to be much lower than normal for someone your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I’ve lost some, yeah. I don’t really think of it as, like, “significant,” but my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to. I’m always pulling them up when I’m on a ladder or bending down, which is annoying more than anything.\n\nAnd people at job sites have said stuff — like, “You’re getting too thin,” or they’ll make comments when I’m carrying supplies, like I look smaller or whatever. I don’t know. I notice it, but I don’t see it the same way they do. To me it feels more like I’ve just leaned out because I’m on my feet all day, going up and down ladders, moving equipment, working long hours. So, yeah, maybe lower than before, but I don’t really know if I’d say “much lower than normal.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if it seems like you're already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a “strong fear” exactly, because that sounds so dramatic, but… yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I eat something that feels really heavy — pizza, or one of those big sandwiches from a deli, something like that — I get this really panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s not even just about how I look, though that’s part of it, I guess. It’s more like I start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m just… bigger, heavier, slower? Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.\n\nSo… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything after eating — like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives — to try to control your weight or keep from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.\n\nLike, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.\n\nI just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. But I can understand that maybe other people would say I put too much importance on it. I don’t know if I fully agree with that, but I can see why you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{"candidates": ["F50.00", "F50.01"], "note": "The patient continues to exhibit concerns about weight and body shape, particularly in relation to their ability to perform physically demanding work and maintain a sense of control over their body and life. They display some features of anorexia nervosa, such as fear of weight gain, restrictive eating patterns, and the influence of body weight on self-evaluation. However, there is no clear evidence of binge eating or purging behaviors, which could support other eating disorder diagnoses. The distinction between F50.00 (Anorexia Nervosa, Restricting Type) and F50.01 (Anorexia Nervosa, Nonrestricting Type) remains uncertain due to lack of clear purging behaviors. No new conditions were ruled out this turn.", "is_final": false}
```

I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.\n\nI just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. But I can understand that maybe other people would say I put too much importance on it. I don’t know if I fully agree with that, but I can see why you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself preoccupied with food, eating, or calories, even when you're not actually eating, in a way that feels hard to control or takes up a lot of your mental energy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / painter_or_paperhanger / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, I think about food, obviously, because everyone has to at some point, and with my job it’s not like I can just eat whenever I want. So I do sometimes have to think ahead, like whether I brought something, or whether I’ll be somewhere I can get something, or if I’m going to be stuck on a site longer than expected.

But “preoccupied” sounds… I don’t know, bigger than how I’d put it. I’m not sitting around doing calorie math all day, at least not in some dramatic way. It’s more like I’m aware of what I’ve had or what I’m planning to have, and sometimes it’s in the back of my mind because I don’t like feeling caught off guard with food. I like knowing what the plan is.

I guess it can take up some mental space, but I don’t know if I’d say it feels hard to control. It feels more like being organized, or trying to avoid making choices when I’m tired or busy. If that makes sense. I don’t know where the line is between normal planning and what you’d call being preoccupied.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because the clinic doctor kind of got on me about my eating. She made it sound like it was a bigger deal than I think it is, but she was pretty insistent that I come talk to someone.\n\nMost days I just keep it simple, you know? I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading out to a job early, and then maybe I’ll take a few bites of something between jobs if I remember or if I have time. I do painting and paperhanging, so the day can get kind of chopped up and messy, and I don’t always want to stop in the middle of everything. Then later I’ll have a small dinner, unless I’m too busy with class paperwork or just trying to get things ready for the next day. It doesn’t feel that unusual to me, but I guess that’s what she was concerned about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you consider your weight to be much lower than normal for someone your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I’ve lost some, yeah. I don’t really think of it as, like, “significant,” but my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to. I’m always pulling them up when I’m on a ladder or bending down, which is annoying more than anything.\n\nAnd people at job sites have said stuff — like, “You’re getting too thin,” or they’ll make comments when I’m carrying supplies, like I look smaller or whatever. I don’t know. I notice it, but I don’t see it the same way they do. To me it feels more like I’ve just leaned out because I’m on my feet all day, going up and down ladders, moving equipment, working long hours. So, yeah, maybe lower than before, but I don’t really know if I’d say “much lower than normal.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if it seems like you're already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a “strong fear” exactly, because that sounds so dramatic, but… yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I eat something that feels really heavy — pizza, or one of those big sandwiches from a deli, something like that — I get this really panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s not even just about how I look, though that’s part of it, I guess. It’s more like I start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m just… bigger, heavier, slower? Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.\n\nSo… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything after eating — like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives — to try to control your weight or keep from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.\n\nLike, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Anorexia Nervosa. to me it feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and busy.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up for jobs and class, but I’ve started avoiding lunches with coworkers or study meetups because it feels easier to keep my routine tight and not have people commenting on what I’m doing.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / painter_or_paperhanger / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S043\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.\n\nI just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. But I can understand that maybe other people would say I put too much importance on it. I don’t know if I fully agree with that, but I can see why you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself preoccupied with food, eating, or calories, even when you're not actually eating, in a way that feels hard to control or takes up a lot of your mental energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about food, obviously, because everyone has to at some point, and with my job it’s not like I can just eat whenever I want. So I do sometimes have to think ahead, like whether I brought something, or whether I’ll be somewhere I can get something, or if I’m going to be stuck on a site longer than expected.\n\nBut “preoccupied” sounds… I don’t know, bigger than how I’d put it. I’m not sitting around doing calorie math all day, at least not in some dramatic way. It’s more like I’m aware of what I’ve had or what I’m planning to have, and sometimes it’s in the back of my mind because I don’t like feeling caught off guard with food. I like knowing what the plan is.\n\nI guess it can take up some mental space, but I don’t know if I’d say it feels hard to control. It feels more like being organized, or trying to avoid making choices when I’m tired or busy. If that makes sense. I don’t know where the line is between normal planning and what you’d call being preoccupied.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these thoughts about food, eating, or your body ever get in the way of your work, relationships, or other important parts of your life, or cause you a lot of distress that’s hard to manage?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not able to do my life. I’m showing up to jobs, I’m getting the painting work done, I’m going to class when I’m supposed to. From the outside, I think it probably looks like I’m handling things.

But it does affect things around the edges, I guess. Like lunches with coworkers — if everyone is going to grab something after being on a job site, I’ve started saying I have errands or I need to get home, because it’s just easier than dealing with food being unplanned or people noticing what I do or don’t order. And with class stuff, if there’s a study meetup at a café or someone’s house where there will be snacks or takeout, I’m more likely to skip it or say I’ll review on my own.

It’s not that I don’t like people. It’s more that keeping my routine tight feels safer and less exhausting. If I can just do things the way I planned, I don’t have to answer questions or feel watched or have someone make some little comment that sticks in my head all day. So yes, I guess it gets in the way socially, even if I’m still technically doing the main responsibilities.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because the clinic doctor kind of got on me about my eating. She made it sound like it was a bigger deal than I think it is, but she was pretty insistent that I come talk to someone.\n\nMost days I just keep it simple, you know? I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading out to a job early, and then maybe I’ll take a few bites of something between jobs if I remember or if I have time. I do painting and paperhanging, so the day can get kind of chopped up and messy, and I don’t always want to stop in the middle of everything. Then later I’ll have a small dinner, unless I’m too busy with class paperwork or just trying to get things ready for the next day. It doesn’t feel that unusual to me, but I guess that’s what she was concerned about.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, or do you consider your weight to be much lower than normal for someone your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess I’ve lost some, yeah. I don’t really think of it as, like, “significant,” but my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to. I’m always pulling them up when I’m on a ladder or bending down, which is annoying more than anything.\n\nAnd people at job sites have said stuff — like, “You’re getting too thin,” or they’ll make comments when I’m carrying supplies, like I look smaller or whatever. I don’t know. I notice it, but I don’t see it the same way they do. To me it feels more like I’ve just leaned out because I’m on my feet all day, going up and down ladders, moving equipment, working long hours. So, yeah, maybe lower than before, but I don’t really know if I’d say “much lower than normal.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if it seems like you're already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a “strong fear” exactly, because that sounds so dramatic, but… yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I eat something that feels really heavy — pizza, or one of those big sandwiches from a deli, something like that — I get this really panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s not even just about how I look, though that’s part of it, I guess. It’s more like I start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m just… bigger, heavier, slower? Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.\n\nSo… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything after eating — like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives — to try to control your weight or keep from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.\n\nLike, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.\n\nI just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. I don’t know. I notice it, but I don’t see it the same way they do. To me it feels more like I’ve just leaned out because I’m on my feet all day, going up and down ladders, moving equipment, working long hours. So, yeah, maybe lower than before, but I don’t really know if I’d say “much lower than normal.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have a strong fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even if it seems like you're already at a low weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a “strong fear” exactly, because that sounds so dramatic, but… yeah, I guess there is something there. Like if I eat something that feels really heavy — pizza, or one of those big sandwiches from a deli, something like that — I get this really panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s not even just about how I look, though that’s part of it, I guess. It’s more like I start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m just… bigger, heavier, slower? Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.\n\nSo… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything after eating — like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives — to try to control your weight or keep from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.\n\nLike, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.\n\nI just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. But I can understand that maybe other people would say I put too much importance on it. I don’t know if I fully agree with that, but I can see why you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself preoccupied with food, eating, or calories, even when you're not actually eating, in a way that feels hard to control or takes up a lot of your mental energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about food, obviously, because everyone has to at some point, and with my job it’s not like I can just eat whenever I want. So I do sometimes have to think ahead, like whether I brought something, or whether I’ll be somewhere I can get something, or if I’m going to be stuck on a site longer than expected.\n\nBut “preoccupied” sounds… I don’t know, bigger than how I’d put it. I’m not sitting around doing calorie math all day, at least not in some dramatic way. It’s more like I’m aware of what I’ve had or what I’m planning to have, and sometimes it’s in the back of my mind because I don’t like feeling caught off guard with food. I like knowing what the plan is.\n\nI guess it can take up some mental space, but I don’t know if I’d say it feels hard to control. It feels more like being organized, or trying to avoid making choices when I’m tired or busy. If that makes sense. I don’t know where the line is between normal planning and what you’d call being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about food, eating, or your body ever get in the way of your work, relationships, or other important parts of your life, or cause you a lot of distress that’s hard to manage?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not able to do my life. I’m showing up to jobs, I’m getting the painting work done, I’m going to class when I’m supposed to. From the outside, I think it probably looks like I’m handling things.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, I guess. Like lunches with coworkers — if everyone is going to grab something after being on a job site, I’ve started saying I have errands or I need to get home, because it’s just easier than dealing with food being unplanned or people noticing what I do or don’t order. And with class stuff, if there’s a study meetup at a café or someone’s house where there will be snacks or takeout, I’m more likely to skip it or say I’ll review on my own.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t like people. It’s more that keeping my routine tight feels safer and less exhausting. If I can just do things the way I planned, I don’t have to answer questions or feel watched or have someone make some little comment that sticks in my head all day. So yes, I guess it gets in the way socially, even if I’m still technically doing the main responsibilities.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I do get upset about it, though I don’t always admit that right away because part of me wants to say it’s just “being organized” or “having a routine.” But if I’m honest, it’s stressful when something involves eating around other people or the plan changes.

Like, I’m still going to work. I’m still showing up to painting jobs, and I’m still going to class. So in my head I tell myself it’s not really interfering because I’m doing the important things. But then there are these smaller things that add up. If coworkers are going to grab lunch after being on a site, I’ll find a reason not to go. I’ll say I have errands or I need to get home or I brought something already. And study meetups are similar — if people are meeting at a café or someone says they’ll order food, I’m much more likely to just skip it and say I’ll review on my own.

It’s not even that I don’t want to be around them. Sometimes I do. I just don’t want to deal with the uncertainty of what food will be there, or feeling like people are noticing what I choose, or don’t choose, or making some offhand comment. Even a small comment can throw me off, and then I’m thinking about it for the rest of the day.

So yes, I guess it does distress me. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from things, but keeping my routine tight feels easier and safer than trying to act normal in those situations.

Like I won’t be able to move the same way at work, climbing ladders or squeezing around furniture or going room to room with supplies. I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ve messed something up and I’m going to feel it in my body right away.\n\nSo… yeah. I don’t sit there using the word “fat” exactly, but I do get scared that eating too much or eating the wrong thing will make me bigger in a way I can’t control.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything after eating — like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives — to try to control your weight or keep from gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I don’t, like, have some whole routine where I eat and then go do something specific right after to “undo” it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, my job is physical anyway — I’m on ladders, carrying drop cloths and tools, moving around rooms, bending and reaching all day — so sometimes if I’ve eaten something heavier, I’ll feel better if I’m busy afterward instead of just sitting there feeling it in my stomach. But that’s kind of just how my days are set up, too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nI don’t really want to get into, like… I don’t know. I guess I’d say I try more to avoid getting to the point where I feel like I need to do something after. Like I’d rather keep things small or simple in the first place, because then I’m not sitting there worrying about it afterward. But no, I’m not sure I’d say I do the specific things you listed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical conditions or been on any medications recently that might explain your weight loss?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I haven’t been told I have some medical thing that would explain it, and I’m not on anything new that I can think of.\n\nI don’t really take much in general, so… yeah, I wouldn’t know what else it would be from. I guess I’ve mostly been assuming it’s just work and being busy and not always stopping for real meals, but no doctor has said, like, “Oh, this medication is doing it,” or “You have this condition.” So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body shape or weight, do you see it differently than others seem to, or feel like people don't understand how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. I don’t think I’m seeing some totally different person in the mirror, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel like other people are reacting to me in a way that feels… exaggerated, honestly.\n\nLike, people keep acting like my weight is this emergency or this huge alarming thing, and I just don’t experience it that way. To me it feels more like I’ve just been disciplined, and busy, and I’m on my feet all day. My work isn’t sitting at a desk, you know? I’m carrying things, climbing, moving around, trying to get jobs done, and I don’t always have time to stop and make a whole production out of eating. So when someone looks at me like I’m about to fall apart, it kind of feels like they’re not understanding the reality of my day.\n\nAnd I know they probably think they’re being concerned, but it can feel almost insulting, like they’re making a big deal out of something that, to me, is just… me keeping myself together and staying in control. I don’t feel like I’m as bad as everyone seems to think. So yeah, I guess I do feel like people don’t really understand how I see it. They see “too thin” or “serious problem,” and I see someone who’s just trying to stay capable and not let things get out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts about your weight or body shape affect how you feel about yourself overall — like, do they play a big role in how you judge your worth or value as a person?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that out, honestly. Like… I don’t wake up thinking, “My value as a person is my weight,” if that’s what you mean. That sounds really extreme when it’s put that way.\n\nBut I guess how I feel in my body does affect my mood. If I feel like I’ve been sloppy or I’ve eaten in a way that makes me feel heavy or uncomfortable, then I don’t feel great about myself. I get kind of irritated with myself, like I should have had better control or planned better. And if I feel lighter or more put together, then I do feel more capable, more like myself.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call that my “worth,” though. I mean, I judge myself on whether I’m working hard, whether I’m reliable, whether I can get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do. But my body is tied into that, because if I feel like I’m not managing it, then it feels like I’m not managing anything. So… maybe it plays a role, but I don’t think of it in those exact words. It’s more like it affects whether I feel in control, and that affects how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body weight or shape influences how you see yourself in a way that feels excessive or disconnected from reality, like it's affecting your sense of who you are more than it should?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, “disconnected from reality” feels like a strong way to put it, and I don’t think that’s how it feels from the inside. It doesn’t feel like I’m walking around with some completely false idea of myself, exactly.\n\nBut… I can see what you’re asking. I guess it probably does influence how I see myself more than I would like to admit. If I feel like my body is under control, then I feel calmer, more competent, more like I can handle things. And if I feel like it isn’t, then it sort of spreads into everything — like I’m not disciplined enough, or I’m letting myself slip. So maybe it affects my sense of myself, yes.\n\nI just have a hard time saying it’s “excessive,” because to me it feels practical. Like, my body is what I use for work, it’s how I get through the day, it’s how I feel whether I’m okay or not. So it doesn’t feel separate from who I am. But I can understand that maybe other people would say I put too much importance on it. I don’t know if I fully agree with that, but I can see why you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself preoccupied with food, eating, or calories, even when you're not actually eating, in a way that feels hard to control or takes up a lot of your mental energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I think about food, obviously, because everyone has to at some point, and with my job it’s not like I can just eat whenever I want. So I do sometimes have to think ahead, like whether I brought something, or whether I’ll be somewhere I can get something, or if I’m going to be stuck on a site longer than expected.\n\nBut “preoccupied” sounds… I don’t know, bigger than how I’d put it. I’m not sitting around doing calorie math all day, at least not in some dramatic way. It’s more like I’m aware of what I’ve had or what I’m planning to have, and sometimes it’s in the back of my mind because I don’t like feeling caught off guard with food. I like knowing what the plan is.\n\nI guess it can take up some mental space, but I don’t know if I’d say it feels hard to control. It feels more like being organized, or trying to avoid making choices when I’m tired or busy. If that makes sense. I don’t know where the line is between normal planning and what you’d call being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about food, eating, or your body ever get in the way of your work, relationships, or other important parts of your life, or cause you a lot of distress that’s hard to manage?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still functioning, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not able to do my life. I’m showing up to jobs, I’m getting the painting work done, I’m going to class when I’m supposed to. From the outside, I think it probably looks like I’m handling things.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, I guess. Like lunches with coworkers — if everyone is going to grab something after being on a job site, I’ve started saying I have errands or I need to get home, because it’s just easier than dealing with food being unplanned or people noticing what I do or don’t order. And with class stuff, if there’s a study meetup at a café or someone’s house where there will be snacks or takeout, I’m more likely to skip it or say I’ll review on my own.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t like people. It’s more that keeping my routine tight feels safer and less exhausting. If I can just do things the way I planned, I don’t have to answer questions or feel watched or have someone make some little comment that sticks in my head all day. So yes, I guess it gets in the way socially, even if I’m still technically doing the main responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel distressed or upset about the way your eating habits or body shape interfere with your ability to be around others, like at meals or gatherings, or when plans don't go the way you expect?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do get upset about it, though I don’t always admit that right away because part of me wants to say it’s just “being organized” or “having a routine.” But if I’m honest, it’s stressful when something involves eating around other people or the plan changes.\n\nLike, I’m still going to work. I’m still showing up to painting jobs, and I’m still going to class. So in my head I tell myself it’s not really interfering because I’m doing the important things. But then there are these smaller things that add up. If coworkers are going to grab lunch after being on a site, I’ll find a reason not to go. I’ll say I have errands or I need to get home or I brought something already. And study meetups are similar — if people are meeting at a café or someone says they’ll order food, I’m much more likely to just skip it and say I’ll review on my own.\n\nIt’s not even that I don’t want to be around them. Sometimes I do. I just don’t want to deal with the uncertainty of what food will be there, or feeling like people are noticing what I choose, or don’t choose, or making some offhand comment. Even a small comment can throw me off, and then I’m thinking about it for the rest of the day.\n\nSo yes, I guess it does distress me. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from things, but keeping my routine tight feels easier and safer than trying to act normal in those situations.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F50.00", "F50.01"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

So yes, I guess it gets in the way socially, even if I’m still technically doing the main responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel distressed or upset about the way your eating habits or body shape interfere with your ability to be around others, like at meals or gatherings, or when plans don't go the way you expect?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I do get upset about it, though I don’t always admit that right away because part of me wants to say it’s just “being organized” or “having a routine.” But if I’m honest, it’s stressful when something involves eating around other people or the plan changes.\n\nLike, I’m still going to work. I’m still showing up to painting jobs, and I’m still going to class. So in my head I tell myself it’s not really interfering because I’m doing the important things. But then there are these smaller things that add up. If coworkers are going to grab lunch after being on a site, I’ll find a reason not to go. I’ll say I have errands or I need to get home or I brought something already. And study meetups are similar — if people are meeting at a café or someone says they’ll order food, I’m much more likely to just skip it and say I’ll review on my own.\n\nIt’s not even that I don’t want to be around them. Sometimes I do. I just don’t want to deal with the uncertainty of what food will be there, or feeling like people are noticing what I choose, or don’t choose, or making some offhand comment. Even a small comment can throw me off, and then I’m thinking about it for the rest of the day.\n\nSo yes, I guess it does distress me. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from things, but keeping my routine tight feels easier and safer than trying to act normal in those situations.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your concerns about your body weight or shape cause you to judge yourself in a way that feels extreme or out of proportion to the actual situation?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

So I do sometimes have to think ahead, like whether I brought something, or whether I’ll be somewhere I can get something, or if I’m going to be stuck on a site longer than expected.\n\nBut “preoccupied” sounds… I don’t know, bigger than how I’d put it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I do judge myself, probably more than I should, but “extreme” is a hard word for me because in the moment it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like I’m just noticing something true, or trying to keep myself from letting things get out of hand.

Like if my clothes feel different or I catch myself at a bad angle, it can really bother me, and then I’ll feel kind of embarrassed that I’m bothered by it. Because logically I know other people probably aren’t paying that much attention, and if a friend said the things to herself that I think sometimes, I’d probably tell her she was being way too harsh. But when it’s me, it feels harder to dismiss.

I don’t know if it’s out of proportion. Maybe sometimes. I think I can take something small and make it mean something bigger about myself, like whether I’m being disciplined enough or whether I’ve messed up somehow. And I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud, but in my head it just sort of happens automatically. So… yes, maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as extreme until later, if at all.
